Estimation and inference of average treatment effects under heterogeneous additive treatment effect model
Xin Lu, Hongzi Li, Hanzhong Liu, Center for Statistical Science, Department of Industrial Engineering

Abstract
Randomized experiments are the gold standard for estimating treatment effects, yet network interference challenges the validity of traditional estimators by violating the stable unit treatment value assumption and introducing bias. While cluster randomized experiments mitigate this bias, they encounter limitations in handling network complexity and fail to distinguish between direct and indirect effects. To address these challenges, we develop a design-based asymptotic theory for the existing Horvitz–Thompson estimators of the direct, indirect, and global average treatment effects under Bernoulli trials. We assume the heterogeneous additive treatment effect model with a hidden network that drives interference. Observing that these estimators are inconsistent in dense networks, we introduce novel eigenvector-based regression adjustment estimators to ensure consistency. We establish the asymptotic normality of the proposed estimators and provide conservative variance estimators under the design-based inference framework, offering robust conclusions independent of the underlying stochastic processes of the network and model parameters. Our method’s adaptability is demonstrated across various interference structures, including partial interference and local interference in a two-sided marketplace. Numerical studies further illustrate the efficacy of the proposed estimators, offering practical insights into handling network interference.



Keywords:
Design-based inference; Direct effect; Global average treatment effect; Indirect effect; Interference; Network interference

1
Introduction

There has been a growing interest in estimating the global average treatment effect (GATE) within randomized experiments
(
Yu et al., 2022
;
Harshaw et al., 2023
)
. The GATE represents the difference in the average outcomes between treating all units versus none in the target population. Under the Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption (SUTVA)
(
Rubin, 1980
)
, the difference in sample means of outcomes under treatment and control is an unbiased estimator of the GATE. However, in many applications, experimental units are interconnected within networks, such as social networks of family, community, or friendship
(
Bond et al., 2012
;
Aral & Nicolaides, 2017
;
Jiang et al., 2023
)
. In such scenarios, empirical evidence has demonstrated the existence of interference, i.e., the treatment assignment of one unit affects the outcomes of others
(
Bond et al., 2012
;
Cai et al., 2015
;
Holtz et al., 2020
;
Leung, 2022
)
. This interference violates SUTVA and may introduce bias to classic Horvitz–Thompson estimator of the GATE. Cluster randomized experiments offer a means to mitigate this bias under appropriate interference assumptions
(
Viviano et al., 2023
;
Chen et al., 2023
;
Holtz et al., 2024
)
.

However, cluster randomized experiments encounter limitations in addressing network interference. Firstly, they necessitate obtaining high-quality graph cuts of the network before experimentation, which is often hindered by the complex structure of real-world networks. For example, the optimal clustering strategy from
Saveski et al. (2017)
accounts for only
35.59\%
of total edges, suggesting that bias remains even after the clustering. Secondly, while cluster randomized experiments are prevalent in social sciences, platforms like eBay, Facebook, and Twitter primarily conduct standard simple randomized experiments, or Bernoulli trials
(
Chin, 2019
)
. Lastly, cluster randomized experiments fail to identify direct and indirect average treatment effects, crucial for various applications such as understanding how policies in one region affect behavior in others
(
Holtz et al., 2020
)
. Thus, there is an urgent need to develop data-adaptive methods for estimating and inferring direct and indirect effects under Bernoulli trials with network interference.

To fill the gap, we develop a novel approach for estimating and inferring the direct average treatment effect (DATE), indirect average treatment effect (IATE), and GATE under the heterogeneous additive treatment effect (HATE) model
(
Sussman & Airoldi, 2017
)
. The HATE model posits that each unit’s outcome is a linear combination of its own treatment assignment and those of its network neighbors, allowing the heterogeneity of indirect effects from different neighbors. This model has gained popularity for incorporating interference
(
Toulis & Kao, 2013
;
Pouget-Abadie et al., 2018
;
Hu et al., 2022
;
Yu et al., 2022
;
Harshaw et al., 2023
;
Chen et al., 2023
;
Holtz et al., 2024
)
within the Neyman–Rubin potential outcomes framework
(
Neyman, 1923
;
Rubin, 1974
)
. We allow the HATE model to accommodate a latent or hidden network that drives interference
(
Yu et al., 2022
)
. For example, in the context of a tech company, users’ social media accounts may form a visible network, while interference is driven by the unobserved network of offline friendships, which only encompasses a fraction of the observed network edges.
Yu et al. (2022)
developed efficient randomized designs to produce an unbiased estimator of GATE when average baseline measurements of outcomes prior to the experiment are available. However, the asymptotic distribution of this GATE estimator remains unknown. Meanwhile,
Hu et al. (2022)
proposed unbiased Horvitz–Thompson estimators for the DATE, IATE, and GATE under a broader interference setting. Nevertheless, the asymptotic properties of these estimators are yet to be explored.

Our contributions are four-fold. Firstly, we formally establish the design-based central limit theory (CLT) for the Horvitz–Thompson estimators of DATE, IATE, and GATE. The design-based inference framework is prevalent in causal inference literature with interference
(
Leung, 2022
;
Aronow & Samii, 2017
;
Hudgens & Halloran, 2008
)
. This framework relies solely on the randomness of treatment assignment, conditioned on the network and parameters underlying the HATE model. Consequently, conclusions drawn from this framework do not depend on the stochastic process underlying the network or model parameters. For achieving this goal, we use the newly developed CLT for homogeneous sums
(
Koike, 2022
)
instead of the traditional CLT that relies on the approach of dependency graph
(
Aronow & Samii, 2017
;
Chin, 2018
;
Harshaw et al., 2023
;
Ogburn et al., 2024
)
. Equipped with this tool, our CLT requires that the average node degree is of magnitude
o(N)
(
N
is the number of experimental units), a notable improvement over the dependency graph approach, which requires a maximum node degree of magnitude
o(N^{1/2})

(
Ogburn et al., 2024
)
.

Secondly, we obtain conservative variance estimators for the Horvitz–Thompson estimators of DATE, IATE, and GATE, facilitating the construction of asymptotically conservative confidence intervals. Compared to the method proposed by
Aronow & Samii (2017)
, our variance estimators offer enhanced tractability and stability, particularly as the average node degree tends to infinity.

Thirdly, we find that the Horvitz–Thompson estimators of IATE and GATE become inconsistent in dense networks (in the sense that the average network degree divided by
\sqrt{N}
does not tend to zero). To address this issue, we propose novel eigenvector-adjusted (EV-adjusted) estimators, ensuring consistency even in significantly dense networks. We establish the asymptotic normality of the EV-adjusted IATE and GATE estimators under the design-based inference framework. The idea of adjusting the eigenvectors was originally proposed by
Li & Wager (2022)
to improve the convergence rates of IATE and GATE estimators in a graphon model. Our eigenvector-based regression adjustment is a model-assisted approach, allowing the graphon model to be misspecified.

Finally, we discuss the application of our method to various interference structures, such as partial interference
(
Sobel, 2006
;
Imai et al., 2021
)
and local interference in a two-sided marketplace
(
Masoero et al., 2024
)
. In the context of partial interference, we demonstrate that the EV-adjusted GATE estimator achieves the same convergence rate as the classic unbiased estimator used in cluster-randomized experiments. Regarding local interference in a two-sided marketplace, we observe that the Horvitz–Thompson estimators lack consistency, while the EV-adjusted estimators maintain consistency and asymptotic normality. These concrete examples highlight the flexibility of our method in handling complex interference patterns.

While several papers have explored GATE estimation in experiments with interference, our work distinguishes itself in several aspects. For example,
Chin (2019)
focused on the super-population framework, while we consider the finite-population or design-based inference.
Harshaw et al. (2023)
proposed a bipartite experimental design, where the units receiving treatment are distinct from those whose outcomes are measured. They studied the estimation of the GATE under a linear exposure model, which requires that the strength of interference is captured by a known weight matrix. In contrast, we consider the Bernoulli trials and the more general HATE model, which allows the strength of interference between units unknown.
Additionally, these studies did not explore the estimation of DATE and IATE. Another line of research employed exposure mapping to define average treatment effects under arbitrary interference, but often relying on stronger assumptions of network structure
(
Aronow & Samii, 2017
;
Leung, 2022
;
Hoshino & Yanagi, 2024
;
Sävje, 2024
)
. For example, both
Aronow & Samii (2017)
and
Hoshino & Yanagi (2024)
assumed that the network possesses bounded node degrees.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
, we introduce the framework and notation including the HATE model. In
Section

3
, we derive unbiased estimators of the DATE, IATE, and GATE and discuss their consistency. In
Section

4
, we establish the design-based central limit theory for these estimators and derive conservative variance estimators. In
Section

5
, we introduce the eigenvector-based regression adjustment method to improve the convergence rates of the IATE and GATE estimators. In
Section

6
, we provide concrete examples illustrating the application and benefits of the regression adjustment methods in three commonly studied interference networks. In Section
7
, we conduct a simulation and real data analysis to evaluate the finite-sample performance of the proposed methods. Lastly, we conclude the paper with a discussion of future research in Section
8
. Proofs are relegated to the Supplementary Material.

2
Framework and notation

Consider a randomized experiment with
N
units where each unit
i
(
i=1,\ldots,N
) is independently assigned a binary treatment
Z_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. Denote
Z_{i}=1
if unit
i
is assigned to the treatment group and
Z_{i}=0
if assigned to the control group. We assume that
Z_{i}\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(r_{1})
for a constant
r_{1}\in(0,1)
determined by the experimenter with
r_{0}=1-r_{1}
. Let
\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
be the vector of treatment assignments for all units. For
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, let
Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})
be the potential outcome
of unit
i
under treatment
\boldsymbol{z}
. The observe outcome of unit
i
satisfies
Y_{i}=Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}I(\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z})Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})
, where
I(\cdot)
is the indicator function. Let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}=\{\boldsymbol{E}=(E_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}:E_{ii}=0,E_{ij}\in\{0,1\},1\leq i,j\leq N\}
denote the set of adjacency matrices of directed network with no self-loop. Suppose that the experimental units interact with each other through an unobserved directed network with a no self-loop adjacency matrix
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{{E}}}
.
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
is a sub-network of an observed directed network
\boldsymbol{E}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}
, i.e.,
\tilde{E}_{ij}\leq E_{ij}
. For example,
\boldsymbol{E}
may represent the social network on Twitter, and
E_{ij}=1
if
i
follows
j
on Twitter. The interference between units is driven by whether
i
and
j
are friends offline, which is coded by
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
. Or
\boldsymbol{E}
may represent the joint network of several candidate networks which might drive the interference and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
is one of the candidate networks.

We are interested in the global average treatment effect (GATE), defined as the difference between the average of outcomes if all units are in the treatment group and that if all units are in the control group:

\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl\{Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{1}_{N})-Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{0}_{N})\bigr\},

where
\boldsymbol{1}_{N}
and
\boldsymbol{0}_{N}
are vectors of all
1
’s and
0
’s of length
N
, respectively.

Let
\boldsymbol{e}_{i,M}
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
with
1
in the
i
th coordinate and
0
elsewhere. For tractable inference of the estimand, we assume throughout that the outcome follows a heterogeneous additive treatment effect (HATE) model
(
Sussman & Airoldi, 2017
)
with a hidden network
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
, that is,

Y_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\theta_{i}Z_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{E}_{ij}\gamma_{ij}Z_{j},

(1)

where
\alpha_{i}=Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{0}_{N})
is the baseline potential outcome if all units are in the control group,
\theta_{i}=Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i,N})-Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{0}_{N})
is the individual level direct effect of unit
i
’s treatment, and
\gamma_{ij}=Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{e}_{j,N})-Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{0}_{N})
,
j\neq i
, is the indirect effect by his friend
j
’s treatment. For
\tilde{E}_{ij}=0
, we have
{\gamma}_{ij}=0
. Here,
\alpha_{i}
,
\theta_{i}
and
\gamma_{ij}
are unobserved latent parameters.

When
E_{ij}=\tilde{E}_{ij}
and
\gamma_{ij}=\beta_{i}v_{ij}
with
v_{ij}
known, the HATE model simplifies to the linear exposure model introduced by
Harshaw et al. (2023)
. Importantly, inferring the GATE under the HATE model does not require consistent estimation of the model parameters such as
\alpha_{i}
,
\theta_{i}
, and
\gamma_{ij}
; see the next section for details.

Remark 1
.

In
Yu et al. (2022)
, the authors addressed the estimation of GATE under the HATE model without access to any information regarding
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
. They opted to define
\boldsymbol{E}
as the most extensive network possible: the complete graph. Their approach necessitates knowledge of the baseline average potential outcome,
N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{0}_{N})
, serving as a surrogate to derive an unbiased estimator for GATE.

Remark 2
.

There are generally two approaches to formalizing interference in the potential outcome framework. The first approach involves constraining the function class of potential outcomes, which often permits a broader range of functions but imposes stringent restrictions on the network structure and interference magnitude (see, for instance, Assumption 6 in
Leung (2022)
and Assumption 4 in
Viviano et al. (2023)
). The second approach entails structural assumptions on the potential outcome model, which incorporate specific domain knowledge regarding the interference pattern. For instance,
Aronow & Samii (2017)
and
Harshaw et al. (2023)
assumed that potential outcomes depend solely on a low-dimensional exposure mapping vector.
Manski (1993)
introduced the linear-in-means model, which suggests a framework where people compare their performance to their peers.
Munro et al. (2021)
explored the scenario of a rival market, where interference is mediated through market prices. Structural assumptions impose tighter constraints on potential outcome models as a trade-off for more intricate network representations or magnitude of interference. The HATE model aligns with the latter approach, offering flexibility to accommodate the complexities of real-world network structures.

Under the HATE model, the GATE is

\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{i}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{E}_{ij}\gamma_{ij}.

Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}=\tilde{E}_{ij}\gamma_{ij}
. Recall that
\tilde{E}_{ii}=E_{ii}=0
, then
\tilde{\gamma}_{ii}=0
. Let
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
and
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
be the average direct and indirect effects, defined as

\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{i},\quad\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}\quad\text{with}\quad\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}+\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}.

Under the HATE model,
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
can be interpreted as the average effect of changing ego treatment from
0
to
1
while keeping alter treatments fixed, while
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
can be interpreted as the average effect of changing alter treatments from zero treated to all treated while keeping ego treatment fixed.

Notation.
For a vector
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
, let
\textrm{diag}(\boldsymbol{a})
be the diagonal matrix with
a_{i}
’s being its diagonal elements. Let
\boldsymbol{1}_{S}
and
\boldsymbol{0}_{S}
be the vectors of all
1
’s and
0
’s of length
S
, respectively. Let
Y_{i}\sim\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
represent the ordinary least squares fit of
Y_{i}
on
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
and we use “
+
” to add more covariate in the regression. Note that we use the regression formula solely to generate the treatment effect estimators. We will evaluate their properties under the design-based inference framework, without imposing linear regression model assumptions.

3
Estimating the average treatment effect

We can estimate the DATE by

\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl\{\frac{Y_{i}Z_{i}}{r_{1}}-\frac{Y_{i}(1-Z_{i})}{r_{0}}\Bigr\}.

Note that
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
is the classic Horvitz–Thompson estimator of the average treatment effect in the no-interference setting
(
Li & Wager, 2022
)
.
Let
Y_{Z_{i}=z}=\operatorname{E}(Y_{i}|Z_{i}=z)
. Define

\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},1}^{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{1}r_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(r_{0}Y_{Z_{i}=1}+r_{1}Y_{Z_{i}=0})^{2},\quad\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},2}^{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}^{2}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}\tilde{\gamma}_{ji}.

Proposition 1
.

\operatorname{E}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})=\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
and
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})=\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},1}^{2}+\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},2}^{2}
.

We now discuss the magnitude of the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
. Let
\tilde{N}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{E}_{ij}
and
{N}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}{E}_{ij}
be the out-degrees of unit
i
in the latent network and observed network, respectively. We posit the following assumption on the magnitude of
\theta_{i}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}
.

Assumption 1
.

There exists a constant
C
independent of
N
such that

\max\Big\{\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}|\alpha_{i}|,\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}|\theta_{i}|,\max_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\tilde{N}_{i}|\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}|\Big\}\leq C.

Assumption

1
bounds the magnitude of the model parameters. Assuming anonymous interference, i.e.,
\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}=\tilde{\gamma}_{i1}
for all
j
with
\tilde{E}_{ij}=1
,
Assumption

1
simplifies to the assumption that
\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}\max_{z\in\{0,1\}^{N}}|Y_{i}(z)|
is bounded, aligning with the standard bounded outcome assumption
(
Aronow & Samii, 2017
;
Leung, 2022
;
Li & Wager, 2022
)
. Furthermore,
Assumption

1
restricts
\max_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\tilde{N}_{i}|\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}|\leq C
, bounding the magnitude of the indirect effect of unit
j
on unit
i
. This constraint is a relaxation of Assumption 4 in
Viviano et al. (2023)
, where they required that
\max_{1\leq i,j\leq N}{N}_{i}|\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}|\min\{N,

\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}N_{i}^{2}\}=o(1)
, translated into our framework.

Define the normalized adjacency matrix of the latent network by
{\boldsymbol{Q}}=(Q_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
with
Q_{ij}=\tilde{E}_{ij}/\tilde{N}_{i}
if
\tilde{E}_{ij}=1
and
0
otherwise. Under Assumption
1
, we have
\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}\leq CQ_{ij}
. Define a density parameter by
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}N_{i}/N^{2}
. Then,
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
is the average number of neighbors (out-degrees) for the units in the observed network. Let
\|\cdot\|_{\textnormal{op}}
be the operator norm of a squared matrix. We consider networks satisfying the following assumptions.

Assumption 2
.

\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(1)
and there exists a constant
C_{+}>0
, such that
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\geq C_{+}
.

Assumption
2
excludes extremely dense networks such as the complete graph and extremely sparse networks such as the edgeless graph. Assumption
3
below imposes regularity conditions on
\boldsymbol{E}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}
.

Assumption 3
.

(i)
\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{\textnormal{op}}=O(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
and (ii)
\|\boldsymbol{Q}\|_{\textnormal{op}}=O(1)
.

Remark 3
.

When the adjacency matrix is symmetric, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}^{\top}
, a sufficient condition for Assumption
3
(i) is that there exists a constant
C_{+}>0
, such that
\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}N_{i}\leq C_{+}N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
, i.e., the maximum degree is bounded by the average degree times a constant. A sufficient condition for Assumption
3
(ii) is that
\max_{i}\tilde{N}_{i}/\min_{i}\tilde{N}_{i}\leq C_{+}
, a uniform degree condition on the latent network; see the Supplementary Material for details.

Proposition
2
below demonstrates the magnitude of the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
.

Proposition 2
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
3
, we have
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})=O(N^{-1})
.

We use the following modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator from
Hu et al. (2022)
for estimating
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
:

\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ij}\Bigl\{\frac{Y_{i}Z_{j}}{r_{1}}-\frac{Y_{i}(1-Z_{j})}{r_{0}}\Bigr\}.

Let
Y_{Z_{i}=z_{i}}^{Z_{j}=z_{j}}=\operatorname{E}(Y_{i}|Z_{i}=z_{i},Z_{j}=z_{j})
, for
1\leq i,j\leq N
and
(z_{i},z_{j})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
. Define

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}^{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{1}r_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Bigl\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}E_{ij}(r_{1}r_{0}Y_{Z_{i}=1}^{Z_{j}=1}+r_{0}^{2}Y_{Z_{i}=0}^{Z_{j}=1}+r_{1}^{2}Y_{Z_{i}=1}^{Z_{j}=0}+r_{1}r_{0}Y_{Z_{i}=0}^{Z_{j}=0})\Bigr\}^{2},

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}^{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\Big(E_{ij}\theta_{i}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}E_{kj}\tilde{\gamma}_{ki}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\Big(E_{ji}\theta_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}E_{ki}\tilde{\gamma}_{kj}\Big)\Big(E_{ij}\theta_{i}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}E_{kj}\tilde{\gamma}_{ki}\Big).

Proposition 3
.

\operatorname{E}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})=\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})=\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}^{2}+\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}^{2}
. Under Assumptions
1
and
3
, we have
\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}^{2}=O(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
and
\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}^{2}=O(\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
.

Proposition

3
shows the unbiasedness of the IATE estimator
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and presents the magnitudes of the two components of the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
. When
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
,
\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}^{2}
is the dominant source of the variance.

We can estimate
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
by a plug-in method:
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}+\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
.
Define

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{1}r_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl\{r_{0}Y_{Z_{i}=1}+r_{1}Y_{Z_{i}=0}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}(r_{1}r_{0}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=1}+r_{0}^{2}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=1}+r_{1}^{2}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=0}+r_{1}r_{0}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=0})\Bigr\}^{2},

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\Big(\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}+E_{ji}\theta_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}E_{ki}\tilde{\gamma}_{kj}\Big)\Big(\tilde{\gamma}_{ji}+E_{ij}\theta_{i}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}E_{kj}\tilde{\gamma}_{ki}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\Big(\tilde{\gamma}_{ji}+E_{ij}\theta_{i}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}E_{kj}\tilde{\gamma}_{ki}\Big)^{2}.

Proposition 4
.

\operatorname{E}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})=\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
and
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})=\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}^{2}+\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2}^{2}.

Under Assumptions
1
–
3
, we have
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}=O(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
, and
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2}=O(\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}).

Proposition
4
implies the unbiasedness of the plug-in estimator
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
and provides the magnitudes of the two components of its variance. By
Proposition

2
,
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
is always
N^{1/2}
-consistent. In other words, one can consistently estimate the DATE regardless of the network density. However, the IATE and GATE estimators may be inconsistent for dense networks. Propositions
3
and
4
implies that
a sufficient condition for the estimators
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
to
be consistent is
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(N^{-1/2})
. Those results echo the findings of
Li & Wager (2022)
.

4
Asymptotic normality and confidence interval

4.1
Central limit theorem

We require the following assumptions to establish the asymptotic normality of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
,
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
, and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
. Let
M_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}
be the in-degree of unit
i
.

Assumption 4
.

\sum_{j=1}^{N}Q_{ji}=o(N^{1/2})
,
\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}N_{i}^{2}=o(N^{3}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
,
\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}M_{i}^{2}=o(N^{3}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
.

By Assumption
3
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\sum_{j=1}^{N}Q_{ji})^{2}=\boldsymbol{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\top}\boldsymbol{1}\leq N\|\boldsymbol{Q}\|_{\textnormal{op}}^{2}=O(N)
,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}N_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ij})^{2}\leq N\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{\textnormal{op}}^{2}=O(N^{3}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji})^{2}

\leq N\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{\textnormal{op}}^{2}=O(N^{3}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}).

Assumption

4
is therefore the Lindberg-type condition, which is a standard condition in the finite-population or design-based inference framework.

Assumption 5
.

\displaystyle(i)\text{ Either}\quad\liminf_{N}N\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},1}>0\quad\text{or}\quad\liminf_{N}N\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},2}>0.

\displaystyle(ii)\text{ Either}\quad\liminf_{N}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}}{N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}}>0\quad\text{or}\quad\liminf_{N}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}}{N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}}>0.

\displaystyle(iii)\text{ Either}\quad\liminf_{N}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}}{N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}}>0\quad\text{or}\quad\liminf_{N}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2}}{N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}}>0.

Assumption

5
includes three parallel assumptions for deriving the asymptotic normality of the DATE, IATE, and GATE estimators, respectively.
Assumption

5
(i) requires that at least one component of
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
does not vanish asymptotically. Since
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}=O(N^{-1})
,
Assumption

5
(i) implies that
\liminf_{N}N\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}>0
, and then the asymptotic distribution is not degenerated.
Assumption

5
(ii) and (iii) have similar interpretations, but for the indirect and global effects, respectively.

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
5
, we have

\frac{\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}}{\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})^{1/2}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1),~~\frac{\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}}{\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})^{1/2}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1),~~\frac{\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}}{\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})^{1/2}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Theorem

1
establishes the central limit theorem for
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
,
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
, and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
. Noteworthy, the CLT holds if
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(1)
(by
Assumption

2
), including the regime where
N^{1/2}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
tends to infinity such that
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
are inconsistent. Our proof is based on the newly developed central limit theory for homogeneous sums
(
Koike, 2022
)
, while the traditional central limit theory relies on the approach of dependency graph
(
Aronow & Samii, 2017
;
Ogburn et al., 2024
;
Harshaw et al., 2023
;
Chin, 2018
)
. As far as we know, the best result obtained by the dependency graph approach requires a strong assumption that the maximum node degree is of magnitude
o(N^{1/2})
, as mentioned by
Ogburn et al. (2024)
.

4.2
Confidence interval

Based on
Theorem

1
, we can construct Wald-type confidence intervals for the average treatment effects as long as we can consistently or conservatively estimate the asymptotic variances. First, we try to estimate
\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},1}^{2}
, which has a nice representation of conditional expectation of outcomes. We ignore the second term for the moment because it involves
N^{2}
nonidentifiable terms and is bounded by or a smaller order of the first term under certain conditions. Note that
Y_{Z_{i}=1}
and
Y_{Z_{i}=0}
are the expected outcomes of unit
i
conditional on it being assigned to the treatment and control groups, respectively. Since we can only observe the outcome of unit
i
under one treatment arm, we cannot consistently estimate the asymptotic variances. Instead, by Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
(r_{1}a_{i}+r_{0}b_{i})^{2}\leq r_{1}a_{i}^{2}+r_{0}b_{i}^{2}
, we obtain an upper bound of the first term of
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})
, which is given by

\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}},1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{Y_{Z_{i}=1}^{2}}{N^{2}r_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{Y_{Z_{i}=0}^{2}}{N^{2}r_{0}}.

Naturally, we use
Y_{i}^{2}I(Z_{i}=z)/\operatorname{E}\{I(Z_{i}=z)\}
to estimate
Y_{Z_{i}=z}^{2}
, which leads to a conservative estimator of
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})
:

\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{Z_{i}Y_{i}^{2}}{N^{2}r_{1}^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{(1-Z_{i})Y_{i}^{2}}{N^{2}r_{0}^{2}}.

Noteworthy,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
is also the variance estimator for the average treatment effect estimator in a no-interference setting.

Theorem 2
.

(i)
\operatorname{E}(\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})=N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{i}^{2}-N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}\tilde{\gamma}_{ji},\\
\operatorname{E}(2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})\geq 0,

and (ii) if Assumptions
1
and
3
holds, then
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}-\operatorname{E}\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1})
.

Theorem
2
(i) characterizes the bias of the variance estimator
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
with respect to
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})
. The estimator
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
, i.e., the classic variance estimator of the Horvitz-Thompson estimator in the no-interference setting multiplied by a factor
2
, serves as a conservative estimator of
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})
. Theorem
2
(ii) shows the consistency of
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
with respect to its expectation
\operatorname{E}\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
. Equipped with a central limit theorem and a variance estimator, we can construct a Wald-type confidence interval.

Remark 4
.

Under the condition discussed below, the second term in
\operatorname{E}(\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})
is negligible compared to the first term, and thus
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
itself is already a conservative estimator. Specifically, if
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
is dense such that each unit is indirectly affected by its sufficiently many neighbors, i.e.,
\min_{i=1,\ldots,N}\tilde{N}_{i}\rightarrow\infty
, then

\operatorname{E}(\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{i}^{2}+o(N^{-1}).

Thus,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
is a conservative estimator of
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}})
. Moreover, since the first term of the bias of
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
is always positive, as shown in our numerical studies,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
is overall conservative even when the assumption
\min_{i=1,\ldots,N}\tilde{N}_{i}\rightarrow\infty
is violated. Therefore, we recommend
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
as the variance estimator to improve the power.

Since
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}
is the main source of variation and it is difficult to estimate
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}
, we derive an estimator for
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}
and ignore
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}
for the moment. By applying Cauchy–Schwarz inequality twice, we derive an upper bound for
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}(r_{1}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=1}+r_{0}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=1})\Bigr\}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}(r_{1}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=0}+r_{0}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=0})\Bigr\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=1}\biggr)^{2}+\frac{r_{0}}{N^{2}r_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=1}\biggr)^{2}+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\frac{r_{1}}{N^{2}r_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=0}\biggr)^{2}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=0}\biggr)^{2}.

Then, we use

\quad\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{I(Z_{j}=z)}{\operatorname{E}I(Z_{j}=z)}\biggr\}^{2}\frac{I(Z_{i}=z^{\prime})}{\operatorname{E}I(Z_{i}=z^{\prime})}

to estimate
\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=z}^{Z_{i}=z^{\prime}})^{2},
which leads to an estimator of
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})
,

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{Z_{j}}{r_{1}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{Z_{i}}{N^{2}r_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{1-Z_{j}}{r_{0}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{r_{0}Z_{i}}{N^{2}r_{1}^{2}}+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{Z_{j}}{r_{1}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{r_{1}(1-Z_{i})}{N^{2}r_{0}^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{1-Z_{j}}{r_{0}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{1-Z_{i}}{N^{2}r_{0}}.

Note that,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
is the weighted sum of squares of treated/control neighbors’ outcome totals.

Theorem 3
.

(i)
\operatorname{E}(2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})\geq 0
.
If further assume Assumptions
1
–
3
holds and
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
, then,

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}\theta_{j}\right)^{2}+o(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}).

(ii) Under Assumptions
1
–
4
, we have
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}-\operatorname{E}(\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
.

Theorem
3
(i) characterizes the bias of
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
with respect to
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})
and demonstrates the conservativeness of
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
. Moreover, when
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
is also a conservative estimator of
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})
.
Theorem

3
(ii) shows the consistency of
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
with respect to its expectation
\operatorname{E}\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
. By
Theorem

1
and
Theorem

3
, the Wald-type confidence intervals based on
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
are asymptotically valid even when
N^{1/2}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(1)
, the asymptotic regime that
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
is inconsistent. However, the confidence intervals may be too wide and lose precision in this case.

Our estimator of
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})
is motivated by the expression of
\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}^{2}
. Analogously, we derive an upper bound for
\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{Z_{i}=1}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=1}\biggr)^{2}+\frac{r_{0}}{N^{2}r_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{Z_{i}=1}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=1}\biggr)^{2}+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\frac{r_{1}}{N^{2}r_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{Z_{i}=0}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=1}^{Z_{i}=0}\biggr)^{2}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{Z_{i}=0}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{Z_{j}=0}^{Z_{i}=0}\biggr)^{2},

and an estimator for this upper bound is

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{Z_{j}}{r_{1}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{Z_{i}}{N^{2}r_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{1-Z_{j}}{r_{0}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{r_{0}Z_{i}}{N^{2}r_{1}^{2}}+

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{Z_{j}}{r_{1}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{r_{1}(1-Z_{i})}{N^{2}r_{0}^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl(Y_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}Y_{j}\frac{1-Z_{j}}{r_{0}}\biggr)^{2}\frac{1-Z_{i}}{N^{2}r_{0}}.

Note that,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
is the weighted sum of squares of treated/control neighbors’ outcome totals plus ego outcomes of treated/control units.

Theorem 4
.

(i)
\operatorname{E}(2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})\geq 0
.
If further assume Assumptions
1
–
3
hold and
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
, then,

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}(\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})-\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\theta_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}\theta_{j}\right)^{2}+o(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}).

(ii) Under Assumptions
1
–
4
, we have
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}-\operatorname{E}\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
.

Theorem
4
(i) characterizes the bias of
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
with respect to
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})
and the conservativeness of
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
. If
N\rho_{N}
, the average number of neighbors, tends to infinity,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
is also a conservative estimator. Theorem
4
(ii) shows the consistency of
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
with respect to its expectation
\operatorname{E}\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
. Similar to IATE, the Wald-type confidence interval based on
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
and
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
is asymptotically valid even in the regime where
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
is inconsistent.

Remark 5
.

Our variance estimators rely on the HATE model.
Aronow & Samii (2017)
developed a general approach for estimating variance, which has been adopted by
Cortez-Rodriguez et al. (2023)
;
Bojinov et al. (2022)
, among others. Specifically, let
\mathcal{N}_{i}
be the set of neighbors of unit
i
,
\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}
represent the subvectors of assignment vectors
\boldsymbol{z}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
corresponding to
i
’s neighbors. If we assume one’s potential outcomes only depend on its neighbors’ treatment vector, their approach involves expressing the estimand and estimators as:

N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{N}_{i}|}}Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}})w_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}})

and expanding the variance as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{N}_{i}|}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{z}_{j}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{N}_{j}|}}Y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i})w_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i})\>Y_{j}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j})w_{j}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j})\cdot

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}\big(I(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}=\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}),I(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{j}}=\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathcal{N}_{j}})\big),

where
w_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}})
is the sample weight of unit
i
with neighbor assignment
\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}
used in the estimand. They then apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and inverse probability weighting to derive a conservative variance estimator. Unlike ours, their estimator lacks a closed-form expression and is computationally inefficient due to the summation of
N^{2}2^{2N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}}
terms. Moreover, their estimator may exhibit poor performance since the inverse probability weighting relies on the probability
\operatorname{E}(I(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{j}}=\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathcal{N}_{j}})I(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}=\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}))
, which tends to
0
as the average degree approaches infinity.
Harshaw et al. (2023)
derived an unbiased variance estimator for the linear exposure model by solving
N^{2}
linear equations. However, their method does not extend to the HATE model and is not applicable when there exists linear equations with no solution.

5
Eigenvector-adjusted estimators of indirect and

global average treatment effect

The consistency of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
requires
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(N^{-1/2})
, i.e., the network is not too dense. To address this issue, we propose an eigenvector regression adjustment method for
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
.
Under appropriate conditions, the proposed method ensures consistency even when
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(1)
.

Let’s first find the reason for the inconsistency of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
when
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
. Let
\boldsymbol{c}=(r_{1}Y_{Z_{1}=1}+r_{0}Y_{Z_{1}=0},\ldots,r_{1}Y_{Z_{N}=1}+r_{0}Y_{Z_{N}=0})^{\top}
. When
N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
and Assumption
1
holds, we can prove that (see Supplementary Materials for details)

\displaystyle\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{1}r_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}(r_{1}Y_{Z_{j}=1}+r_{0}Y_{Z_{j}=0})\Bigr\}^{2}+O(\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{1}r_{0}}\boldsymbol{c}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{c}+O(\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}).

Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{N}
be the ordered eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
with corresponding eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{V}=(\boldsymbol{V}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{V}_{N})
, that is,

\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}=(\boldsymbol{V}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{V}_{N})\textrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})(\boldsymbol{V}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{V}_{N})^{\top}.

If the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
are spiked, then the variance explosion is primarily caused by
\boldsymbol{c}
that is correlated with the dominant eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
, i.e., the eigenvectors corresponding to large eigenvalues. Meanwhile, note that
\boldsymbol{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{1}/N^{2}=N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2}
. Strong correlation between
\boldsymbol{c}
and
\boldsymbol{1}
also induces a large variance. Similar discussion applies to
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})
.
Based on the above observation, we propose to adjust the correlation between
Y_{Z_{i}=z}
and the dominant eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
as well as
\boldsymbol{1}
to reduce the variance of the IATE estimator. Specifically, let
(\boldsymbol{V}_{1},\ldots\boldsymbol{V}_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K}
be the
K
dominant eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
. We regress
Y_{i}
on
(\boldsymbol{1},\boldsymbol{V}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{V}_{K})
for
\{i:Z_{i}=1\}
and
\{i:Z_{i}=0\}
, respectively. Let
V_{ik}
be the
i
th element of
\boldsymbol{V}_{k}
for
k=1,\ldots,K
, and let
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}=(1,V_{i1},\ldots,V_{iK})^{\top}
. The Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) regression coefficient is

\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{z}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(Y_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i})^{2}I(Z_{i}=z).

Let
\hat{e}_{i}=Y_{i}-\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}}I(Z_{i}=z)\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
be the residual of the OLS regression. The EV-adjusted IATE and GATE estimators are defined as

\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ij}\Bigl\{\frac{\hat{e}_{i}Z_{j}}{r_{1}}-\frac{\hat{e}_{i}(1-Z_{j})}{r_{0}}\Bigr\},\quad\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}=\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}+\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}.

Remark 6
.

\boldsymbol{V}_{k}
represents the estimated membership indicator vector for community
k
in the stochastic block model and the estimated eigencomponent of a graphon model
(
Li & Wager, 2022
)
. We can interpret the regression with
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
as adjusting the outcome for the main effect and the community effect. Importantly, we adopt the design-based inference framework, and thus, the theoretic results allow the stochastic block model to be misspecified. Let
\mathcal{N}=\{1,\ldots,N\}
be the population set. If the network has several components with no between-cluster edges and
\cup_{h=1}^{H}\mathcal{S}_{h}=\mathcal{N}
with
\mathcal{S}_{h}\cap\mathcal{S}_{h^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, for all
h\neq h^{\prime}
. We can also adjust the effect of the components by using
(I(i\in\mathcal{S}_{1}),\ldots,I(i\in\mathcal{S}_{H}))_{i=1}^{N}
in the regression.

Since linear regression yields the same output
\hat{e}_{i}
under linear transformation of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, without loss of generality, we normalize
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
such that
N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{I}_{K+1}
. Next, we derive the asymptotic distributions of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
. Define the oracle regression coefficient for
z\in\{0,1\}
as
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{z,\textrm{ora}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}Y_{Z_{i}=z}/N,
with the oracle residual

\displaystyle e_{i}

\displaystyle=Y_{i}-Z_{i}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}-(1-Z_{i})\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i})+(\theta_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i})Z_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}Z_{j}.

Note that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,\textrm{ora}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,\textrm{ora}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}(Y_{Z_{i}=1}-Y_{Z_{i}=0})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\theta_{i}.

Therefore,
\theta_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
is the OLS residual of regressing
\theta_{i}
on
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
. Let
\theta_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}=\theta_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,\textrm{ora}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
,
e_{Z_{i}=z_{i}}^{Z_{j}=z_{j}}=\operatorname{E}(e_{i}\mathrel{|}Z_{i}=z_{i},Z_{j}=z_{j})
, and
e_{Z_{i}=z_{i}}=\operatorname{E}(e_{i}\mathrel{|}Z_{i}=z_{i})
.
The oracle EV-adjusted IATE and GATE are defined as

\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ij}\Bigl\{\frac{{e}_{i}Z_{j}}{r_{1}}-\frac{{e}_{i}(1-Z_{j})}{r_{0}}\Bigr\},\quad\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}=\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}+\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}.

It’s easy to see that
\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})
is the same as
\textnormal{Var}(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})
appeared in
Proposition

3
except that
\theta_{i}
and
Y_{Z_{j}=z_{j}}^{Z_{i}=z_{i}}
are replaced with
\theta_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}
and
e_{Z_{j}=z_{j}}^{Z_{i}=z_{i}}
, respectively. The same is true for
\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
. Let
\boldsymbol{c}_{e}=(r_{1}e_{Z_{1}=1}+r_{0}e_{Z_{1}=0},\ldots,r_{1}e_{Z_{N}=1}+r_{0}e_{Z_{N}=0})^{\top}
and
\alpha_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}
be the OLS residual of regressing
\alpha_{i}
on
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}=\alpha_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\alpha}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{i},\quad\text{where}\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\alpha_{i}.

We define
h_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}
analogously as the OLS residual of regressing
h_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}{\tilde{\gamma}}_{ij}
on
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
. Let

\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=N^{-1}\max\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}e_{Z_{j}=1}\Big)^{2},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}e_{Z_{j}=0}\Big)^{2}\Big\}.

Here,
\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
plays an important role in the variance of EV-adjusted estimators. We can show that
\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=O(N^{2}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
under
Assumption

3
.

Assumption 6
.

There exists a constant
C
independent of
N
, such that

\max\big\{\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|\alpha_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}|,\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|\theta_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}|,\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|h_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}|\big\}\leq C.

Assumption

6
is the analog of
Assumption

1
extended to the adjusted parameters.
Define
(\sigma^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1})^{2}
and
(\sigma^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1})^{2}
similarly to
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}
and
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}
with
Y_{Z_{j}=z}^{Z_{i}=z^{\prime}}
(
(z,z^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
) replaced by
e_{Z_{j}=z}^{Z_{i}=z^{\prime}}
. Similarly, define
(\sigma^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2})^{2}
and
(\sigma^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2})^{2}
by replacing
\theta_{i}
with
\theta_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},i}
in
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}
and
\sigma^{2}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2}
, respectively.
Proposition
5
below gives the formulas and magnitudes of the variances of EV-adjusted IATE and GATE estimators.

Proposition 5
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
3
and
6
, we have

\displaystyle\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})=(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}+(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}=O(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}),

\displaystyle\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})=(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}+(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}=O(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}).

Remark 7
.

Given that
e_{Z_{i}=z}
are orthogonal to the first
K
eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
, and
\max_{i}\max_{z\in{0,1}}|e_{Z_{i}=z}|\leq C_{e}
as implied by
Assumption

6
, where
C_{e}
is a constant independent of
N
, we can derive
N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=O(\lambda_{K+1}/N)
.

Assumption 7
.

(i)\text{ Either}\quad\liminf\frac{(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},1}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}}{N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}}>0\quad\text{or}\quad\liminf\frac{(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},2}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}}{N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}}>0.

(ii)\text{ Either}\quad\liminf\frac{(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},1}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}}{N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}}>0\quad\text{or}\quad\liminf\frac{(\sigma_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}},2}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{2}}{N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}}>0.

Assumption 8
.

N^{-1}\max\{\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}e_{Z_{j}=1})^{2},\max_{i=1,\ldots,N}(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}e_{Z_{j}=0})^{2}\}=o(\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}).

Assumption 9
.

There exists a constant
C_{\boldsymbol{W}}
independent of
N
, such that
\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\boldsymbol{W}}
.

Assumption
7
is analogous to
Assumption

5
on the variances of the EV-adjusted estimators. Assumption
8
is a Lindeberg-type condition for deriving the asymptotic normality of the EV-adjusted estimators.
Assumption

9
requires
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
to be uniformly bounded. Informally, if we view
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
as the estimated community membership or the component membership,
Assumption

9
requires the size of each estimated community or component used in the regression to be comparable to the population size
N
. For example, as in
Remark

6
, if we have a component
\mathcal{S}_{h}
of size
o(N)
, we should not directly use
(I(i\in\mathcal{S}_{h}))_{i=1}^{N}
in the regression.

Theorem 5
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
4
and
6
–
9
, we have
(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}})/\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{1/2}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
(\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}})/\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})^{1/2}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Theorem

5
establishes the asymptotic normality of the EV-adjusted estimators
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
. Their asymptotic variances are of order
N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
(see
Proposition

5
), representing an improvement in convergence rates over their unadjusted counterparts. In cases where
N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(1)
, as demonstrated in the examples in the subsequent section, we can ensure the consistency of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
provided that
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=o(1)
. Consequently, the EV-adjusted estimators relax the assumptions on the network structure compared to the unadjusted Horvitz–Thompson estimators.

To estimate the asymptotic variances and construct confidence intervals, we modify
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
by replacing the treated/control neighbors’ outcome (
Y_{i}
) totals with their residual
(
\hat{e}_{i}
) totals, and we denote the resulting variance estimators as
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
and
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
.

Theorem 6
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
4
,
6
, and
8
–
9
, we have (i)
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
and
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
, (ii)
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})=R_{N}^{(1)}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
and
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})=R_{N}^{(2)}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
,
where
R_{N}^{(1)}\geq 0
and
R_{N}^{(2)}\geq 0
,
and (ii) if further assume that
\liminf N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}/\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\rightarrow\infty
, then

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}\theta_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},j}\right)^{2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}\Big(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\Big),

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\theta_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{ji}\theta_{\backslash\boldsymbol{W},j}\right)^{2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}\Big(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\Big).

Theorem

6
(i) presents the magnitude of the variance estimators.
Theorem
6
(ii) demonstrates that
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
and
2\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
are conservative variance estimators. Moreover,
\hat{V}_{\star}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
(
\star\in\{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}\}
) exhibits a smaller size of
O_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}+\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
compared to the
O_{\mathbb{P}}(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{2})
of
\hat{V}_{\star}
, thereby enhancing the precision of inference. Theorem
6
(iii) implies that the second term in the variances
\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})
and
\textnormal{Var}(\tilde{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}})
becomes negligible compared to the first term if
N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
tends to
0
slower than
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
. In such cases,
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
and
\hat{V}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
already serve as conservative variance estimators.

6
Application example

In this section, we illustrate, by three concrete examples, that the EV-adjusted estimators can significantly improve the estimation precision and the inference efficiency, demonstrating their flexibility to handle various interference structures.

6.1
Partial interference

Partial interference refers to interference occurring solely among units within the same group
(
Sobel, 2006
;
Imai et al., 2021
;
Baird et al., 2018
)
. Under partial interference, saturation designs, an extension of cluster-randomized experiments, prove useful for estimating both direct and spillover effects
(
Baird et al., 2018
;
Imai et al., 2021
)
. This involves randomly assigning clusters with varying treatment proportions. We explore the use of Bernoulli trials for estimating treatment effects and demonstrate their efficiency, showing that the EV-adjusted GATE estimator under Bernoulli trials can achieve the same convergence rate as the Horvitz–Thompson estimator of the GATE under cluster-randomized experiments.

Suppose the target population is divided into
M
non-overlapping, equally-sized groups (clusters), each containing
N_{M}
units, resulting in a total population size of
N=MN_{M}
. For ease of representation, let
\{(m-1)N_{M}+1,\ldots,mN_{M}\}
denote group
m
. We denote by
C_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
the group indicator of unit
i
, where
C_{i}=m
if
i\in\{(m-1)N_{M}+1,\ldots,mN_{M}\}
. Each group forms a complete graph, and thus the whole network is composed of components of complete graphs, each of size
N_{M}
, with no between-group edges. Partial interference aligns with Assumption
10
below, where each group corresponds to a complete graph.

Assumption 10
.

For
i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
E_{ij}=1
if
C_{i}=C_{j}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{J}_{N_{M}}
denote the square matrix of all ones with dimensions
N_{M}\times N_{M}
. Under partial interference, the adjacency matrix takes the form of a block diagonal matrix
\textrm{diag}(\{\boldsymbol{J}_{N_{M}}-\boldsymbol{I}_{N_{M}}\}_{m=1}^{M})
. The density of the network is
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=M\binom{N_{M}}{2}/\binom{N}{2}=O(M^{-1})
. Corollaries
1
and
2
below provide the convergence rates for the unadjusted and EV-adjusted IATE and GATE estimators.

Corollary 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
3
and
10
, we have
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{1/2}M^{-1})
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{1/2}M^{-1})
.

Recall from Theorem
2
that the convergence rate of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{DIR}}}
is
N^{-1/2}
, regardless of the network structure. Therefore, to consistently estimate
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
using
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
, we require
N_{M}=o(N^{1/2})
. An alternative approach to estimating GATE under partial interference involves conducting cluster-randomized experiments, where each group (cluster) receives the same treatment. The difference in means of outcomes under the treatment and control is an unbiased estimator of GATE, with a convergence rate of
O_{\mathbb{P}}(M^{-1/2})
. This rate is faster than that of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
unless
N_{M}=O(1)
.

It is well known that the spectrum of a complete graph is
(N_{M}-1)^{1}
and
(-1)^{N_{M}-1}
, where the exponent denotes the multiplicity. Therefore, the spectrum of this block diagonal matrix is
(N_{M}-1)^{M}
and
(-1)^{N-M}
. Let
\boldsymbol{e}_{m,M}
be the
m
-th canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
. The
M
dominant eigenvectors are
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{m,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{M}}\}_{m=1}^{M}
, where
\otimes
is the Kronecker product. Here,
\boldsymbol{e}_{m,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{M}}
is the vector of the group membership indicator of group
m
. The variances of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
are primarily attributed to the correlation between
Y_{i}
and these eigenvectors. Therefore, it is intuitive to use
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}=(I(C_{i}=1),\ldots,I(C_{i}=M))^{\top}
in the regression.

We cannot adjust for all the
M
eigenvectors because our theory restricts us to using a fixed number of eigenvectors. Moreover, adjusting the effect of all groups violates
Assumption

9
, which requires the size of each group to be comparable with
N
, i.e.,
N_{M}\geq CN
for some constant
C>0
. To address this issue, we assume the between-groups homogeneity (see
Assumption

11
below) and then we can merge
M
group indicators into a single indicator, leading to
\hat{e}_{i}
obtained from

\displaystyle Y_{i}\sim I(Z_{i}=0)+I(Z_{i}=1).

(2)

Assumption 11
.

N_{M}^{-1}\sum_{i:C_{i}=m}Y_{Z_{i}=z}=N_{M}^{-1}\sum_{i:C_{i}=1}Y_{Z_{i}=z}
, for all
m=1,\ldots,M
,
z=0,1
.

Remark 8
.

We can further relax
Assumption

11
. Specifically, if we can partition the
M
groups into several strata and assume between-group homogeneity within each stratum, we can merge the cluster indicators into a stratum indicator and perform regression using the stratum indicator; see the Supplementary Material for details.

Using the residual of regression (
2
) to construct
\hat{\tau}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
, we obtain their convergence rates, as shown in
Corollary

2
below.

Corollary 2
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
4
and
6
–
11
, we have
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(M^{-1/2})
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(M^{-1/2})
.

By
Corollary

2
,
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
achieves the same convergence rates as the difference in means estimator under cluster-randomized experiments. Meanwhile, we can estimate the DATE and IATE, which are not identifiable under cluster-randomized experiments.

Under
Assumption

11
, the oracle residual
e_{i}
of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\equiv 1
is the same as that of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\equiv(I(C_{i}=1),\ldots,I(C_{i}=M))^{\top}
. Thereby, we actually adjust the first
M
eigenvectors of the graph using
Equation

2
(See Supplementary Materials for more details).
Subsequently, following the argument of
Remark

7
, we find that
\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=O(\lambda_{M+1})=O(1)=o(N_{M}^{-1})=o(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
.
By
Proposition

5
,
\hat{\tau}_{\star}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
,
\star\in\{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}\}
improves the convergence rate of
\hat{\tau}_{\star}
, from
N^{1/2}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
to
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{1/2}
.

6.2
Local interference in a two-sided marketplace

There is a growing interest in estimating GATE in experiments carried out in a two-sided marketplace
(
Masoero et al., 2024
;
Holtz et al., 2024
;
Harshaw et al., 2023
)
. The target population has a matrix form. Consider a
N_{R}\times N_{C}
matrix with
N=N_{R}N_{C}
where each row represents a buyer and each column represents a seller. We observe outcomes for the
(r,c)
-th pair under a treatment targeted to that specific pair, such as the amount of money the
r
-th buyer spends with the
c
-th seller, with or without the implementation of an encouragement policy. Equipped with the EV-adjusted estimators, we aim for valid inferences in Bernoulli trials under local interference assumption in a two-sided marketplace, where the interference exists within rows and columns.

Let
C_{i}
and
R_{i}
be the column and row numbers of the
i
-th pair, where
C_{i}=c
and
R_{i}=r
if
i=(r-1)N_{R}+c
,
1\leq i\leq N
. Local interference assumes that interference occurs solely within units in the same column (same seller) or row (same buyer). We then translate this assumption into the language of networks.

Assumption 12
.

For
i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
E_{ij}=1
if
C_{i}=C_{j}
or
R_{i}=R_{j}
.

Under
Assumption

12
,
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=\big\{N_{R}\binom{N_{C}}{2}+N_{C}\binom{N_{R}}{2}\big\}/N^{2}=O(N_{R}^{-1}+N_{C}^{-1})
. Hence, Assumption
2
requires that
N_{R},N_{C}\rightarrow\infty
.
Corollary

3
below presents the convergence rates of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
.

Corollary 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
3
, and
12
, we have
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{1/2}N_{R}^{-1}+N^{1/2}N_{C}^{-1})
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{1/2}N_{R}^{-1}+N^{1/2}N_{C}^{-1})
.

The consistency of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
requires that
\max\{N_{R},N_{C}\}=o(N^{1/2})
, which is impossible. Therefore, we need to use the EV-adjusted estimators. It is not difficult to show that

\boldsymbol{E}=\sum_{r=1}^{N_{R}}(\boldsymbol{e}_{r,N_{R}}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{C}})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{e}_{r,N_{R}}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{C}})+\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{R}}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{c,N_{C}})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{R}}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{c,N_{C}})-2\boldsymbol{I}_{N}.

Therefore, the dominant eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}
are in the space spanned by
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{r,N_{R}}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{C}}\}_{r=1}^{N_{R}}\cup\{\boldsymbol{1}_{N_{R}}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{c,N_{C}}\}_{c=1}^{N_{c}}
, which are the column and row indicator vectors. Choosing the first row and first column as the reference level, it is intuitive to use
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}=(1,I(C_{i}=2),\ldots,I(C_{i}=N_{C}),I(R_{i}=2),\ldots,I(R_{i}=N_{C}))^{\top}
in the regression adjustment. This regression contains
2(N_{R}+N_{C}-1)
parameters with
2(N_{R}+N_{C}-1)\rightarrow\infty
and the corresponding eigenvectors violate
Assumption

9
. To reduce the number of regressors and fulfill
Assumption

9
, we assume homogeneity between columns and rows and perform regression (
2
). Then, we use the regression residual to construct
\hat{\tau}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
with convergence rates being presented in
Corollary

4
below.

Assumption 13
.

For all
c=1,\ldots,N_{C}
and
r=1,\ldots,N_{R}
,

\displaystyle N_{R}^{-1}\sum_{i:C_{i}=c}Y_{Z_{i}=z}=N_{R}^{-1}\sum_{i:C_{i}=1}Y_{Z_{i}=z},\quad N_{C}^{-1}\sum_{i:R_{i}=r}Y_{Z_{i}=z}=N_{C}^{-1}\sum_{i:R_{i}=1}Y_{Z_{i}=z}.

Remark 9
.

Similar to
Remark

8
, we can relax
Assumption

13
, if we can group sellers and buyers, and assume both between-group buyer-homogeneity and between-group seller-homogeneity. The corresponding regression is a
two way effect linear regression; see the Supplementary Materials for more details.

Corollary 4
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
4
,
6
–
9
, and
12
–
13
, we have
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N_{R}^{-1/2}+N_{C}^{-1/2})
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}-\tau_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(N_{R}^{-1/2}+N_{C}^{-1/2})
.

Corollary

4
indicates that the EV-adjusted estimators
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
are still consistent if
N_{R},N_{C}\rightarrow\infty
. The oracle residual
e_{i}
of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\equiv 1
is the same as that of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}\equiv(1,I(C_{i}=2),\ldots,I(C_{i}=N_{C}),I(R_{i}=2),\ldots,I(R_{i}=N_{C}))^{\top}
. Therefore, regression (
2
) adjusts the first
N_{R}+N_{C}-1
top eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}
and
\lambda_{N_{R}+N_{C}}=(-2)^{2}=O(1)
, and we have
\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}=O(\lambda_{N_{R}+N_{C}})=O(1)=o(N\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}})
. Therefore,
\hat{\tau}_{\star}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}
,
\star\in\{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}},\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}\}
improves the convergence rate of
\hat{\tau}_{\star}
, from
N^{1/2}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
to
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{1/2}
.

6.3
Random graph drawn from a graphon model

Li & Wager (2022)
considered random graphs generated from a graphon model. To see the magnitude of
\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
and the convergence rates of the IATE and GATE estimators, we assume that the observed network comes from a graphon model in this subsection.

In the graphon model, let
U_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textnormal{Uniform}[0,1]
be a latent random variable associated with the
i
-th unit. Assume that
E_{ij}=E_{ji}
and conditional on
U_{i}
’s,
E_{ij}
is generated through
E_{ij}\sim\textnormal{Bernoulli}(G_{N}(U_{i},U_{j}))
for
i<j
, where
G_{N}:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow[0,1]
is called the graphon function. We consider
G_{N}(U_{i},U_{j})=\min\{1,\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star}G(U_{i},U_{j})\}
and focus on a setting where
G(U_{i},U_{j})
has a low rank representation:

G(U_{i},U_{j})=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\lambda_{k}\psi_{k}(U_{i})\psi_{k}(U_{j}).

Li & Wager (2022)
proposed the following regularity assumptions on the graphon function.

Assumption 14
.

(i) The function
g_{1}(u)=\int_{0}^{1}\min(1,G(u,t))dt
is bounded away from
0
, i.e.,
g_{1}(u)\geq C_{+}
for a constant
C_{+}>0
and any
u\in[0,1]
;
(ii)
|\lambda_{1}|\geq|\lambda_{2}|\geq\cdots\geq|\lambda_{r}|>0
,
\operatorname{E}\{\psi_{k}(U_{1})^{2}\}=1
and
\operatorname{E}\{\psi_{k}(U_{1})\psi_{l}(U_{1})\}=0
for
k\neq l
; (iii)
\psi_{k}(U_{1})
satisfies the Bernstein condition with parameter
b
, i.e., for
k=2,3,4,\ldots
,

|\operatorname{E}\{\psi_{k}(U_{1})-\operatorname{E}\psi_{k}(U_{1})\}^{k}|\leq\frac{1}{2}k!\textnormal{Var}\{\psi_{k}(U_{1})\}b^{k-2};

and (iv)
\liminf\log\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star}/\log N>-1/2
and
\liminf\log\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star}/\log N<0
.

Assume that the rank
r
of the graphon is known. Then we use the dominant
r
eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}
as
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
in the regression adjustment.
Then, we use the residual of the above regression to construct
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
.

Proposition 6
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
6
, and
14
, with probability tending to one, we have, for any
\delta>0
,

N^{-1}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\leq C(\log N)^{4+\delta}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star}
and
\underline{C}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star}\leq\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}\leq\bar{C}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star}
for constant
C,\underline{C},\bar{C}>0
.

Recall that conditional on the graph the EV-adjusted estimators have the magnitude of
O_{\mathbb{P}}(\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{1/2}+N^{-1/2}\Delta_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{1/2})
. Therefore,
by
Proposition

6
, under such a graphon model,
\hat{\tau}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\scalebox{0.6}{{EV}}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
improve the convergence rates of
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{IND}}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}
, from
N^{1/2}\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star}
to
(\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}^{\star})^{1/2}
(up to a log factor) if we use the top
r
eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{E}
.

7
Numerical study

7.1
Numerical experiments

In this section, we conduct numerical simulations to evaluate the performance of the proposed estimators for DATE, IATE, and GATE, and to verify the improvement in estimation accuracy and inference efficiency of the EV-adjusted estimators. We consider three different scenarios: partial interference, local interference, and interference based on a real-world network. For comparison, we also present the results of methods from existing literature under these scenarios.

Consider a Bernoulli trial with treatment probability
r_{1}=0.5
. The outcomes are generated by the heterogeneous additive treatment effect model (
1
), that is,

Y_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\theta_{i}Z_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{E}_{ij}\gamma_{ij}Z_{j}.

The parameters are generated from normal distribution and
t
-distribution. Formally, we generate
\alpha_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ i.i.d. }}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},1)
,
\theta_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ i.i.d. }}}{{\sim}}0.8\cdot t_{3}(\mu_{i},0.5)
and
\gamma_{ij}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ i.i.d. }}}{{\sim}}1.8\cdot t_{3}((\mu_{i}+\mu_{j})/2,0.5)
, where
t_{3}(\mu,\sigma)
denotes the
t
-distribution with 3 degrees of freedom, location parameter
\mu
and scaling parameter
\sigma
. To meet Assumption
1
, we normalize the indirect effect parameters by
\gamma_{ij}=\gamma_{ij}^{\prime}/\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{E}_{ij}
. The sample size
N
, the network structure, and the parameters
\mu_{i}
’s are specified differently in the following three scenarios:

Scenario 1
(Partial interference): Consider the network structure described in Section
6.1
. The outcomes are generated through a hidden network. Specifically, we generate the observed network
\boldsymbol{E}
by Assumption
10
, and then independently remove each edge in
\boldsymbol{E}
with a probability of 0.75 to obtain
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
. We set the cluster size
N_{M}
and the number of clusters
M
to be
(10,45)
and
(20,180)
. The numbers of observed neighbors for each unit are 9 and 19, and the average numbers of true neighbors for each unit are 2.25 and 4.75, respectively. The observed network densities
\rho_{\lx@scalerel@obj{N}}
are approximately 0.020 and 0.005. As mentioned in Remark
8
, suppose that all clusters can be equally divided into 3 strata, where the parameter
\mu_{i}
is set to be the same within each stratum, and takes values
0,1,2
for different strata. In this setting,
\mu_{i}
actually represents the stratum effect. We use the cluster stratum indicators as the eigenvectors in regression adjustment. As mentioned in Remark
4
, we employ the variance estimators without multiplication by 2 for inference (the same in the other two scenarios).

In partial interference case, a common method to estimate the GATE is to perform cluster randomization, which means a Bernoulli trial at the cluster level. The simple difference in means between treated and control clusters (denoted as
\hat{\tau}_{\scalebox{0.6}{{TOT}}}^{\textnormal{CL}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}Z_{i}Y_{i}/(Nr_{1})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}(1-Z_{i})Y_{i}/(Nr_{0})
) can be used as the estimator
(
Viviano et al., 2023
)
. If we consider clusters as aggregated units and the cluster-averaged outcomes as corresponding outcomes, then this estimator is equivalent to our estimator for the direct effect (defined on aggregated units). Therefore, similar normal approximation and Neyman-type variance estimators can be used for inference. We set the treatment probability of cluster randomization to 0.5 as well. To fit the finite-population framework, we generate the observed network, the hidden network, and the parameters
(\alpha_{i},\theta_{i},\gamma_{ij})
once, then separately repeat the Bernoulli trial and cluster randomization 10000 times to evaluate the bias, standard deviation (SD), root mean squared error (RMSE) of the point estimators, and empirical coverage probability (CP) and mean confidence interval length (Length) of
95\%
confidence intervals.
